Personalized Multi-Agent Average Reward TD-Learning via Joint Linear Approximation
Leo Muxing Wang, Pengkun Yang, Lili Su

Abstract
We study personalized multi-agent average reward TD learning, in which a collection of agents interacts with different environments and jointly learns their respective value functions. We focus on the setting where there exists a shared linear representation and the agents’ optimal weights collectively lie in an unknown linear subspace. Inspired by the recent success of personalized federated learning (PFL), we study the convergence of cooperative single-timescale TD learning in which agents iteratively estimate the common subspace and local heads. We showed that this decomposition can filter out conflicting signals, effectively mitigating the negative impacts of “misaligned” signals, and achieve linear speedup. The main technical challenges lie in the heterogeneity, the Markovian sampling, and their intricate interplay in shaping error evolutions. Specifically, not only are the error dynamics of multiple variables are closely interconnected, but there is also no direct contraction for the principal angle distance between the optimal subspace and the estimated subspace. We hope our analytical techniques can be useful to inspire research on deeper exploration into leveraging common structures. Experiments are provided to show the benefits of learning via a shared structure to the more general control problem.



1
Introduction

In many real-world applications, ranging from simple assistive robotics to autonomous vehicles, autonomous agents operate in different local environments.
For example, consider the simple robot vacuums. Their interacting environments are mostly shaped by the household conditions, which can vary significantly in floor plans, obstacle types (e.g., moving humans and furniture), and spatial configurations. Similarly, autonomous vehicles deployed across different regions of a metropolitan area may encounter substantial variability in road conditions and traffic patterns. Such environmental heterogeneity can lead to misaligned learning signals across agents, which may significantly hinder convergence and impair generalization of joint learning.
Nevertheless, applying standard single-agent reinforcement learning (RL) solutions may lead to overall intensive, yet possibly redundant, computation and sample collection. This is because the cumulative knowledge obtained by one agent may still be partially useful to others when some common structure (though unknown) exists.
These two competing intuitions raise a natural question:

Question: Do the benefits of collaboration outweigh the drawbacks – or is it the other way around?

Existing studies of multi-agent RL (MARL) with heterogeneous environments are mostly restricted to training a common policy or value-/Q-function
(
18
;
37
;
41
;
47
)
, and the optimality is often described with respect to some imaginary environment constructed as a weighted average of the agents’ state-transition kernels
(
18
)
.
Though this approach has practical value – particularly when environmental heterogeneity is mild – in many applications (such as on-device recommender systems
(
22
)
and smart healthcare
(
23
)
), heterogeneity is often moderate to severe, which can significantly degrade the performance of a common policy.
In a parallel line of research on multi-agent Markov games or competitive MARL, learning personalized (or localized) policies or value-/Q-functions has recently garnered considerable attention
(
14
;
46
;
27
;
48
;
9
)
. Specifically, in their setting, agents interact with one another within a shared environment, and the rewards received by individual agents are determined by the joint actions of all agents. It is easy to see that in such a setup, the more agents there are, the more complicated an agent’s decision problem becomes. In fact, this line of work often suffers from the so-called curse of multi-agents—the sample complexity increases linearly or even exponentially
(
29
;
17
;
46
)
with respect to the number of agents.

In this paper, we take a step toward addressing the above question by studying cooperative temporal-difference (TD) learning, where a collection of agents collaboratively learn their heterogeneous value functions through local TD updates.
Inspired by the recent success of personalized federated learning (PFL) and multi-task learning, we focus on the specific underlying common structure that the agents’ optimal weights under a shared linear representation collectively lie in a low-dimensional linear subspace
(
35
;
8
;
33
;
12
;
11
;
34
;
24
)
. Our contributions can be summarized as

•

We propose and analyze the convergence of a cooperative average-reward TD method in which agents iteratively estimate the common subspace and agent-specific heads. We show that the overall reward estimate errors decay at a rate of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/T)
, where
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}
hides polylog factors of T, with a faster stepsize. Our dynamics of common subspace
{\mathbf{B}}
and local heads
\omega^{k}
are single timescale, i.e., their respective stepsizes are on the same order with respect to
T
. We show that, when
T
is sufficiently large, under additional conditions, the joint estimation errors of the subspace and the local heads converge to 0 at a rate
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{TLK}})
, where
L
is the number of local steps and
K
is the number of agents.

•

Our analysis is highly nontrivial, which may be insightful for a broader audience dealing with coupled heterogeneous dynamics. Technically, the Markovian sample of TD error makes it difficult to obtain a direct contraction for the principal angle distance between the optimal subspace and the estimated subspace, but only an indirect subtraction in terms of the local weights error.
To address this challenge, we show that the local weights error can be lower-bounded by the principal angle distance times a constant that depends on the diversity of the optimal local weights.

•

We empirically validate that the proposed personalized framework achieves superior performance compared to other frameworks, including single-agent, universal-policy, and two-timescale approaches, with respect to convergence speed, stability, and generalization.

During the preparation of this paper,
(
42
)
studied discounted TD learning with two timescales and established a linear convergence speedup. Key differences are as follows: We study average-reward TD and we study the single timescale dynamics of the local heads and shared common spaces.
However, under their notation, the validity of the error contraction in Eq. (54) appears to rely on the assumptions
D_{1}=o(\beta_{t})
and
D_{2}=o(\alpha_{t+1})
. Since both terms involve stepsize ratios such as
\frac{\beta_{t}}{\alpha_{t}}
and
\frac{\beta_{t}}{\alpha_{t+1}}
, these conditions implicitly require a sufficiently strong separation between the two stepsize sequences. While such assumptions are standard in two-timescale analyses, they are not automatically guaranteed for commonly used polynomial stepsizes and may require additional verification. In contrast, our analysis establishes error contraction under a single-timescale setting and directly controls the coupled error terms through a unified Lyapunov argument, without relying on such asymptotic separation conditions.

2
Related Work

MARL under heterogeneous environments.

There is an emerging interest in mathematically understanding the role of environmental heterogeneity in the performance of the federated versions of classic reinforcement learning algorithms
(
18
;
37
;
41
;
47
)
, yet most existing studies focus on training a common policy or approximating a common function.

(
18
)
studied federated Q-learning and policy gradient methods, assuming known transition kernels.

(
38
)
studied federated Q-learning with unknown transition kernels, and found that, in the presence of environmental heterogeneity, the eventual convergence rate may depend solely on the number of communication rounds – multiple updates within each round cannot accelerate convergence.

(
37
)
proposed FedTD(0) with linear function approximation, and linear convergence speedup in a low-heterogeneity regime.

(
41
)
used the KL-divergence to penalize the deviation of local update from the global policy, and proved that under the setting of heterogeneous environments, the local update is beneficial for global convergence using their method. Yet, no convergence rate is provided.
(
45
)
proposed FedSARSA and proved that the algorithm can converge to a near-optimal solution.
Neither
(
41
)
nor
(
45
)
characterized sample complexity.

Personalization in heterogeneous environments.

Training personalized policies in cooperative multi-agent systems in the presence of environment heterogeneity remains largely underexplored.

(
44
)
studied the training of personalized policies in the context where each agent has a private reward function but shares a common transition kernel for the environment. The analysis focused solely on the convergence of the global policy, defined as the average of the local policies.

(
18
)
proposed a heuristic deep FedRL method, where the policy network can be decomposed into a shared subnetwork and environment embedding layer.

(
42
)
studied personalized TD learning with two timescales and established a linear convergence speedup.

3
Problem Setup and Preliminaries

Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning.

We consider a multi-agent system that consists of one parameter server and
K
agents.
The agents are modelled as Markov Decision Processes (MDP)
\{{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\mid{\mathcal{M}}^{k}=<P^{k},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}},R>\text{ for }k=1,2,...,K\}
, where
{\mathcal{S}}
is the (possibly infinite) state space,

{\mathcal{A}}
is a finite action space,
P^{k}
’s are the transition kernels, and
R
is the reward function:
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow[-U_{r},U_{r}]
.
The environments
P^{k}
with which the agents interact are heterogeneous.
The parameter server can orchestrate the learning at the agents through iteratively aggregating the updates at the agents.

We study average-reward reinforcement learning
(
36
)
, which provides a principled framework for maximizing the mean reward. Even in the single-agent setting, unlike discounted and finite-horizon MDPs, the sample complexity of average-reward settings remains largely unexplored
(
16
)
. In this paper, we consider the more challenging multi-agent setting. In particular, we study personalized multi-agent average-reward TD learning.

Fix a policy
\pi
. We assume that, for each agent
k\in[K]
, the limiting state distribution induced by the transition kernel
P^{k}
under policy
\pi
exists; we denote this distribution by
\mu^{k}
.
The time-average expected reward at agent
k
is defined as:

\displaystyle J^{k}(\pi)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{T}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}R(s^{k}_{t},a^{k}_{t})\right]

(1)

\displaystyle=\underset{(s,a)\sim\mu^{k}\otimes\pi}{\mathbb{E}}[R(s,a)],

where
(s^{k}_{t},a^{k}_{t})
is the state-action pair of agent
k
at iteration
t
, for
t=0,1,\cdots
.
The first expectation in Eq. (
1
) is taken over the state-action trajectories generated under the Markov chain, while the second is taken over their limiting distribution. Here, the notation
\mu^{k}\otimes\pi
denotes sampling
s\sim\mu^{k}
and
a\sim\pi(\cdot\mid s)
.

The value function is defined as

\displaystyle V^{k,\pi}(s)={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}R(s^{k}_{t},a^{k}_{t})-J^{k}(\pi)\mid s_{0}=s\right].

(2)

For ease of exposition, we drop the index and function argument in
\pi
. That is, for each
k
,
J^{k}(\pi)
is written as
J^{k}
, and
V^{k,\pi}(s)
as
V^{k}(s)
for short.

Single-agent TD Learning.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a function space that
V^{k}
belongs to.
Let
\phi:{\mathcal{S}}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
be a feature map of
{\mathcal{V}}
.
Denote the coefficients of the value
V^{k}
with respect to
\phi
as
z^{k,*}
, where
z^{k,*}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
. For simplicity, we assume
\phi
is known and has finite dimension
d
, i.e.,
\phi:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Consequently,

V^{k}(s)=\left\langle\phi(s),z^{k,*}\right\rangle,~~~~\forall~s\in{\mathcal{S}},\text{and }k\in[K].

We consider the following linear function approximation:

\widehat{V}(s;z^{k})=\phi(s)^{\top}z^{k}.

To drive the approximation
\widehat{V}(s;z^{k})
to
V^{k}
,
single-agent TD learning (particularly TD(0))
update is applied at an agent to learn its value function:

\displaystyle z^{k}_{t+1}

\displaystyle=z^{k}_{t}+\beta\bigg(R(a_{t}^{k},s_{t}^{k})-\eta_{t}^{k}

(3)

\displaystyle+\left(\phi(s^{k}_{t+1})-\phi(s^{k}_{t})\right)^{\top}z^{k}_{t}\bigg)\phi(s_{t}^{k}),

\displaystyle\eta_{t+1}^{k}

\displaystyle=\eta_{t}^{k}+\gamma\left(R(a_{t}^{k},s_{t}^{k})-\eta_{t}^{k}\right),

where
\beta
and
\gamma
are stepsizes, and
\eta_{t}^{k}
is the reward estimator.
In literature,
\delta_{t}^{k}:=R(a_{t}^{k},s_{t}^{k})-\eta_{t}^{k}+\left(\phi(s^{k}_{t+1})-\phi(s^{k}_{t})\right)^{\top}z^{k}_{t}
is often referred to as TD(0) error.
It is well-known that under some standard technical assumptions
\lim_{t\to\infty}z^{k}_{t}\to z^{k,*}

(
36
;
30
)
.

Multi-agent TD Learning: Commonality and Heterogeneity.

In multi-agent TD learning, the
K
agents aim to collaboratively learn their respective value functions under the given
\pi
based on their collective state-action trajectories.
Learning with shared representation is widely recognized as an effective way to separate commonalities from heterogeneity across various heterogeneous data
(
4
;
1
;
2
;
20
;
24
)
.

Inspired by the literature of personalized FL and multi-task learning,
following
(
35
;
8
;
33
;
12
;
11
;
34
;
24
)
,
we assume that
\{z^{k,*}:~k=1,\cdots,K\}
belongs to and fully span an
r
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
That is, there exists an orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
such that

\displaystyle z^{k,*}=\mathbf{B}^{*}\omega^{k,*},~~~\text{where}~~\omega^{k,*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}.

(4)

To see the generality of this formulation:
r=1
when
z^{k,*}=z^{k^{\prime},*}
for all
k,k^{\prime}
(i.e., homogeneous transitional kernels
P^{k}
);
r=K
when
z^{k,*}\perp z^{k^{\prime},*}
for all
k^{\prime}\not=k
– which is possible when
K\leq d
.

4
Algorithm: PMAAR-TD

We propose a personalized multi-agent average-reward TD learning algorithm (
PMAAR-TD
) based on joint linear approximation, in which agents iteratively estimate the underlying truth subspace
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
and their respective local “heads”
\omega^{k,*}
.
Specifically,
\{z^{k}={\mathbf{B}}\omega^{k}\}_{k\in[K]}
are decomposed into the product of the subspace estimate
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
, which is common over all agents, and an agent-specific head
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
.

A formal description can be found in Algorithm
1
. At a high level, in each iteration, each agent locally performs the TD(
L
) update, and uses the obtained TD(
L
) error
\delta_{t,L}^{k}
– formally defined in Eq. (
6
) – and rewards to update estimates of its local head
\omega^{k}
, common subspace
{\mathbf{B}}
, and local reward
\eta^{k}
.
The updates of
\omega^{k}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
can be interpreted as performing a perturbed stochastic gradient on the following joint linear approximation problem:

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathbf{B}},\omega^{1},\cdots,\omega^{K}}~\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{k}}[(\widehat{V}^{k}(s;\omega^{k},\mathbf{B})-V^{k}(s))^{2}],

(5)

\displaystyle\text{where}~~\widehat{V}^{k}(s;\omega^{k},\mathbf{B})=\phi(s)^{\top}\mathbf{B}{\omega^{k}}.

A natural stochastic gradient descent of Eq. (
5
) updates of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
\omega^{k}
for all
k
are:

\displaystyle\omega_{t+1}^{k}=\omega_{t}^{k}+\beta\left[V^{k}(s_{t,0}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})^{\top}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}{\omega_{t}^{k}}\right]{\mathbf{B}_{t}}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}=\mathbf{B}_{t}^{k}+\zeta\left[V^{k}(s_{t,0}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})^{\top}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}{\omega_{t}^{k}}\right]\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top},

where
\beta
and
\zeta
are stepsizes of
\omega^{k}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
, respectively.
However,
V^{k}
is unknown for each
k
. Following the semi-gradient method TD(
L
), we use

\displaystyle R(s_{t,0}^{k},a_{t,0}^{k})-J^{k}+R(s_{t,1}^{k},a_{t,1}^{k})-J^{k}+...

\displaystyle\qquad+R(s_{t,L-1}^{k},a_{t,L-1}^{k})-J^{k}+\widehat{V}^{k}(s_{t,L}^{k};\omega_{t}^{k},\mathbf{B}_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}R(s_{t,\ell}^{k},a_{t,\ell}^{k})-J^{k}+\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})^{\top}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}{\omega_{t}^{k}}

to estimate
V^{k}(s_{t,0}^{k})
. The
L
-step TD error at each agent is

\displaystyle\delta_{t,L}^{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}r_{t,\ell}^{k}-\eta_{t}^{k}+(\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}{\omega_{t}^{k}},

(6)

where
r_{t,\ell}^{k}=R(s_{t,\ell}^{k},a_{t,\ell}^{k})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}=\omega_{t}^{k}+\beta\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}),

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}=\mathbf{B}_{t}+\zeta{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}.

(8)

Algorithm 1

PMAAR-TD

1:

Input:
Initial heads
\{\omega_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
; initial subspace
{\mathbf{B}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
, which is orthonormal; three stepsizes:
\beta
for local heads,
\zeta
for subspace, and
\gamma
for reward estimator; total iteration
T
; projection upper bound
U_{\omega}
such that
\|\omega^{k,*}\|\leq U_{\omega}
for
k\in[K]
.

2:

for

k=1,2,\dots,K

do

3:

\eta_{0}^{k}=0

4:

Draw
s_{0,0}^{k}
arbitrarily from
{\mathcal{S}}

5:

end

for

6:

for

t=0,1,2,\dots,T-1

do

7:

for

k=1,2,\dots,K

do

8:

\delta_{t,L}^{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}(r_{t,\ell}^{k}-\eta_{t}^{k})+(\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}
.

9:

\omega^{k}_{t+1}=\Pi_{U_{\omega}}\left(\omega^{k}_{t}+\beta\frac{1}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\right)
.

10:

\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}=\mathbf{B}_{t}+\zeta\mathbf{B}_{t,\perp}\mathbf{B}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\frac{1}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega^{k}_{t})^{\top}
.

11:

\eta^{k}_{t+1}=\eta^{k}_{t}+\gamma\left(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}r_{t,\ell}^{k}-\eta^{k}_{t}\right)
.

12:

s_{t+1,0}^{k}=s_{t,L}^{k}
.

13:

end

for

14:

\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}
.

15:

\mathbf{B}_{t+1},\mathbf{R}_{t+1}=\texttt{QR}(\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1})
.

16:

Server broadcasts
\mathbf{B}_{t+1}
to all agents.

17:

end

for

Nevertheless, Eq. (
7
) and Eq. (
8
) are not the final updates to obtain
\omega_{t+1}^{k}
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}
.
Several key algorithmic components
are incorporated to provide fine-grained control of the perturbation and to enforce contraction of the principal-angle distance between
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
– addressing major technical challenges.

(A) Projection operation on local heads
\prod_{U_{\omega}}
: This operation project
\omega
onto a convex ball with radius
U_{\omega}
. Unlike existing fine-grained analyses, such as in
(
19
)
, the dynamics of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
\omega^{k}
(one for each agent), together with
\eta_{t}^{k}
, are highly coupled due to the personalization. Without relying on any absolute bound on
\omega^{k}
, characterizing the error dynamics quickly becomes intractable. Despite this technical convenience, it introduces “not so small” perturbation that interplays with the errors of other dynamics nontrivially that can be harmful to the convergence and potential speedup.

(B) Subspace projected innovation in Eq. (
8
): Instead of let
{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}^{k}
towards the local innovation
\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}
, we move
{\mathbf{B}}
in the direction of the “residual” innovation that lies outside of
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
. This projection significantly mitigates some otherwise amplified perturbations arising from multiple cross terms. For example, under this projection, the perturbation in the QR decomposition is only second order; see Lemma
C.1
.

(C) QR: Under this decomposition,
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
is orthonormal for each
t
, which is a key structural property that contributes to the contraction of the principal-angle distance between
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}

(
8
)
.

It is worth noting that
(
42
)
does not use the above algorithmic details to control the perturbation.
This is because that the challenge of single-timescale (of
{\mathbf{B}}
and
\omega^{k}
) convergence analysis for this problem is that the estimation errors of the critic and the subspace misalignment are strongly coupled, whereas in double-loop or two-timescale analysis, these error terms are usually naturally decoupled.

5
Convergence Analysis

We present some technical assumptions adopted in our convergence analysis.
Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
are widely adopted in the RL literature.
Assumption
4
quantifies how well spread the underlying truth
z^{k,*}
is in covering the
r
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
.

Assumption 1

(Exploration)
.

The matrix

A_{L}^{k}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(s^{(0)})(\phi(s^{(L)})-\phi(s^{(0)}))^{\top}],

where the expectation is taken with respect to
s^{(0)}\sim\mu^{k},(a^{(\ell)},s^{(\ell+1)})\sim\pi\otimes P^{k}~\forall\ell\in[0,L-1]
, is negative definite and its largest eigenvalue is upper bounded by
-L\lambda
.

This assumption is widely adopted in analyzing TD learning with linear function approximation
(
6
;
3
;
5
;
25
;
26
;
40
;
49
)
.
Intuitively,
A_{L}^{k}
captures the exploration of the policy
\pi
under the transition kernel
P^{k}
within
L
steps. For the tabular setting, Assumption
1
holds when the policy
\pi
explores all state-action pairs with
L
steps.

Assumption 2

(Uniform ergodicity)
.

Let
{\mathbb{P}}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s)
denote the state distribution after
\tau
steps given
s_{0}^{k}=s
for agent
k
. There exist
m>0
and
\rho\in(0,1)
, such that at all
k\in[K]
,

d_{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s),\mu^{k}(\cdot))\leq m\rho^{\tau},~\forall\tau\geq 0,s\in{\mathcal{S}},

(9)

where
d_{TV}
is the total variation distance.

In our analysis, with a little abuse of notation, we also use
{\mathbb{P}}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s)
, which denotes the state-action pair at time
\tau
given
s_{0}^{k}=s
.
Recall that
\mu^{k}
is the limiting distribution induced by policy
\pi
and the transition kernel
P^{k},\forall k
. Uniform ergodicity is often assumed to characterize the noise induced by Markovian sampling
(
6
;
25
;
49
;
40
)
.

Furthermore, we assume bounded features.

Assumption 3
.

\|\phi(s)\|\leq 1
for each
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

This assumption is rather standard in RL literature
(
31
;
30
;
6
;
25
;
49
;
40
)
.
The existence of a shared common subspace helps to reduce the local learning problem from a
d
-dimensional problem to an
r
-dimensional problem. In PFL, when
r\ll d
, this benefits are well characgterized and are indeed substantial in derived bounds
(
8
;
24
)
. Under Assumption
3
, however, the dependence on
d
is masked, potentially obscuring these gains in the resulting bounds. Adopting more fine-grained assumptions could therefore lead to significantly sharper results. Developing such refinements, however, would require a fundamental rethinking of the problem and is left for future work.

Let

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Z}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times K},~~~\text{with }z^{k,*}~~\text{as the $k$-th column}.

(10)

By Eq. (
4
), we know that the rank of
{\mathbf{Z}}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}^{*\top}
is
r
. We require that each of the
r
dimensions of
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
is well-covered by
\{z^{k,*}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, formally stated in Assumption
4
.

Assumption 4
.

\frac{1}{K}\lambda^{+}_{\min}({\mathbf{Z}}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}^{*\top})\geq\alpha
for some
\alpha>0
.

In our analysis, we also use
\lambda_{\min}^{+}=\lambda_{\min}^{+}({\mathbf{Z}}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}^{*\top})
to denote the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
{\mathbf{Z}}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}^{*\top}
.

5.1
Main Convergence Results

In Algorithm
1
, three groups of variables are updated: the common subspace estimate
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
, the local heads
\omega_{t}^{k}
, and estimates of local time-averaged rewards (defined in Eq. (
1
)). In this subsection, we characterize their evolutions over time. For ease of exposition, we introduce a set of notation:

\displaystyle x_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{B}}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}-z^{k,*},~m_{t}={\mathbf{B}}^{*\top}_{\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},~y_{t}^{k}=\eta_{t}^{k}-J^{k},

(11)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\omega}_{t}^{k}=\omega^{k}_{t}+\beta\delta_{t,L}^{k}\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}),

(12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{t}^{k}=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t}\widetilde{\omega}_{t}^{k}-z^{k,*},\quad\quad\bar{m}_{t}={\mathbf{B}}^{*\top}_{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t}.

(13)

Fix
\tau
.
For any
t\geq\tau
, define

M_{t}={\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|m_{t}\|_{F}^{2},\quad\bar{X}_{t}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2},

where
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}
is the conditional expectation, conditioning on the randomness up to time
t-\tau
.

Lemma 5.1

((informal) Upper bound of local head errors )
.

Let
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}={\mathbf{B}}^{*}({\mathbf{B}}^{*})^{\top}
, and
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}={\mathbf{B}}_{t}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}
for each
t
. Choose
\tau=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
,
U_{\delta}U_{\omega}\zeta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, where
U_{\delta}=2U_{r}+2U_{\omega}
,
\beta=c\zeta
for arbitrary
c>0
that can be tuned. There exist arbitrary positive constants
c_{3},c_{4},c_{5},c_{6}
such that

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{t+1}\leq\Bigg(1-2\lambda c\zeta+\Big(c_{3}^{2}\frac{c}{L}+c_{4}^{2}c+c_{5}^{2}\frac{1}{L}+c_{6}^{2}+4U_{\omega}^{4}\frac{1}{c_{5}^{2}L}\Big)\zeta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{2})\zeta^{2}+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{2})\zeta^{3}\Bigg)\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{36U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{3}^{2}}\frac{c\zeta}{L}+6U_{\omega}U_{\delta}c\zeta\right)M_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Bigg(\Big[\frac{1}{c_{4}^{2}}c+\frac{U_{\omega}^{4}}{c_{6}^{2}}\Big]\zeta+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{2})\zeta^{2}+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{2})\zeta^{3}\Bigg)\bar{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,5}(\tau^{2})\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L}+{\mathcal{C}}_{X,6}(\tau^{2})\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K}+O(\zeta^{3})+O(\gamma^{2}\zeta^{2}),

where

{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{2}),{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{2}),{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{2}),{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{2}),{\mathcal{C}}_{X,5}(\tau^{2}),
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,6}(\tau^{2})
are on the order of
\tau^{2}
, formally defined in Appendix
D.1
.

Intuitively, when
M_{t}\lesssim\bar{X}_{t}
. The above upper bound is similar to the traditional single-agent setting
(
6
)
.

Deriving Lemma
5.1
is highly nontrivial and requires a fundamental detour from the existing analysis
(
6
)
. Furthermore, existing analysis in PFL
(
8
)
is not applicable to our problem due to the Markovian sampling, the TD updates, and the lack of responses.
Roughly speaking, the main drift of the decay in
\|x_{t}^{k}\|
may arise from
\langle x_{t}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\delta_{t,L}^{k}\rangle
.
However, the environmental heterogeneity significantly complicates the characterization of this term. Specifically,

\displaystyle\langle x_{t}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\delta_{t}^{k}\rangle=\langle x_{t}^{k},{\mathbf{P}}_{t}A_{L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}\rangle+\text{``perturbation''}.

Zooming into this expression, the main negative drift arises from the term
\langle x_{t}^{k},{\mathbf{P}}_{t}A_{L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}\rangle
. Intuitively, in traditional single-agent or homogeneous environment settings,
\langle x_{t}^{k},{\mathbf{P}}_{t}A_{L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}\rangle\approx\langle x_{t}^{k},A_{L}x_{t}^{k}\rangle
, which is mainly controlled by the spectrum of
A_{L}
, with the desired property directly assumed in Assumption
1
. However, in the presence of environmental heterogeneity, Assumption
1
does not directly guarantee a negative drift of the
x_{t}^{k}
due to the existence of
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}
and its intricate interplay with the heterogeneous
A_{L}^{k}
.
Specifically, (1)
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}
is not of full rank, (2) the local head error will be distorted in a different manner due to the product
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}A_{L}^{k}
, and (3)
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}
varies over time.

We further show that it is impossible for
M_{t}\gg\bar{X}_{t}
. In fact, a slightly stronger result holds.

Lemma 5.2

(Lower bound of local head errors)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2r
. It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{\|m_{t}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda^{+}_{\min}({\mathbf{Z}}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}^{*\top}).

Intuitively, Lemma
5.2
says that when the principal angle distance between
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
is large, the well coverage of all the
r
directions by the underlying truth
\{z^{1,*},\cdots,z^{K,*}\}
ensures that the aggregate errors in the local head estimates remain bounded away from zero.
In other words, the local heads cannot be learned significantly faster than the subspace itself.

Lemma 5.3

(Principal angle distance analysis)
.

Choosing
\beta=c\zeta
for some
c>0
, and
\zeta
so that
U_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}\zeta^{2}\leq\frac{1}{6}
, when
d\geq 2r
, there exist arbitrary positive
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
such that

\displaystyle M_{t+1}\leq(1-\frac{\lambda\alpha\zeta}{r}+c_{1}^{2}\zeta+c_{2}^{2}\zeta)M_{t}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{32U_{\omega}^{2}\zeta^{2}}{L^{2}}+\frac{16U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{2}^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{L^{2}}+4c_{3}^{2}\frac{\zeta}{L^{2}}\right)\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle+\left(8U_{\omega}^{2}\zeta^{2}+\frac{4U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{1}^{2}}\zeta+4c_{4}^{2}\zeta\right)\bar{Y}_{t}+{\mathcal{C}}_{M,1}\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K}+{\mathcal{C}}_{M,2}\zeta^{6}

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{C}}_{M,3}\zeta^{4}+{\mathcal{C}}_{M,4}\zeta^{3}+4C_{15}\tau C_{5}c\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\sqrt{L}}

\displaystyle+4C_{14}\tau C_{10}\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\sqrt{K}}+4C_{19}(\tau)\gamma\zeta^{2},

where the missing specification of the (nearly) constant terms can be found in the analysis. They either are problem-specific constants or depends only on problem-specific consants and
\tau
.

Lemma 5.4

(Reward analysis)
.

Fixed a
\tau
.
For
t>\tau
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(y_{t}^{k})^{2}\leq(y_{0}^{k})^{2}e^{-2\gamma t}+\frac{\gamma}{2-\gamma}4U_{r}^{2}+8U^{2}_{r}m\rho^{\tau}(1-\gamma)^{\tau}

\displaystyle\qquad+8U_{r}^{2}\tau\gamma(1-\gamma)^{\tau}+8U_{r}^{2}\frac{\tau(\tau-1)}{2}\gamma^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4|y_{0}^{k}|U_{r}e^{\gamma\tau}e^{-\gamma 2t}+4U_{r}(1-\gamma)^{T}|y_{0}^{k}|m\rho^{\tau}.

Choosing
\tau\leq\min\{\frac{2T}{\log(T)},\lceil\log_{\rho}(\gamma)\rceil,\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil\}
and
\gamma=\frac{\log(T)}{2T}
, the average reward can be bounded as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(y_{T}^{k})^{2}\leq\frac{28U_{r}^{2}}{T}+\frac{8U_{r}^{2}\tau\log(T)}{T}+\frac{8U_{r}m\log(T)}{T}.

Lemma
5.4
says that reward estimation error decays at the rate of
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{1}{T})
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
hides the polylog factor of
T
. It is worth noting that in Algorithm
1
the reward learning at different clients are independent of each other, and the dynamics of
{\mathbf{B}}
and local heads
\omega^{k}
do not affect
y^{k}
.
Though there is no averaging over agents, the reward learning itself can be made faster than the learning of subspace and local heads. With minor changes, the same analysis of Lemma
5.4
can be extended to the conditional expectation, such as
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}(y_{t}^{k})^{2}
for sufficiently large
t
.

Theorem 1

(Convergence (informal))
.

Consider Algorithm
1
with
\beta=c\zeta,\zeta=\frac{4r}{\sqrt{T}\lambda\alpha},\gamma=\frac{\log(T)}{2T}
, where
c>0
and satisfies
\frac{\alpha}{4r}\leq c\leq\frac{\alpha}{2r}
,
K>0
satisfies
\max\left\{\frac{30}{\lambda K},\frac{120U_{\omega}^{4}}{\lambda K}\right\}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2r},L=K.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{T}=\bar{X}_{T}+\kappa M_{T}
be the Lyapunov function, where
\kappa\geq\frac{2(1080U_{\omega}^{2}+6U_{\omega}U_{\delta}\lambda L^{2})}{\lambda^{2}L^{2}}=\Theta(1)
.
Let
\tau=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
.
We have for
T\geq 2\tau
, and for sufficiently large
K

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{T}=\widetilde{O}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{(TK)K}})+{\mathcal{O}}(\frac{\log(T)}{T}),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
hides the polylog factors.

When
T
is sufficiently large, the first term dominates, which is a linear speedup. The threshold of sufficiency of “large”
L
or
K
(recalling that we choose
K=L
) can be found in Eq. (
95
), which is independent of
T
.

6
Experiments

6.1
Numerical Experiments on Prediction Problems

In this section, we evaluate the performance of
PMAAR-TD
for approximating value functions under a fixed policy. We compare our proposed approach,
PMAAR-TD
, against the baselines introduced in Sections
3
:
Single TD
and
FedTD-Uniform
.
Single TD
is the standard single-agent TD learning;
FedTD-Uniform
periodically averages the parameters of all agents and outputs a universal value function for all agents across different environments. The following is the specifications of how to evaluate the performance of value function approximation:

1.

A pre-trained single-agent TD model, trained with standard environment parameters, is employed as the fixed policy under which the value function is evaluated.

2.

A fixed initial state is selected and consistently applied across all methods throughout the entire duration of the experiment.

3.

Value function convergence is monitored by periodically evaluating the preselected initial state via a forward pass, utilizing the resulting value estimate as a proxy for training progress. We adopt this scheme because the state space is continuous, and the design ensures that the preselected state appears in every training episode.

The value of the preselected state is recorded during training serves as a metric to quantify the convergence rate toward the optimal value function for each approach. For the experiments, we utilize three distinct seeds and a synchronization rate of
{E=50}
as shared hyperparameters. The specific configuration of the Acrobot environment for each agent is detailed below:

We analyze the results presented in Figure
1
from two primary perspectives:

1.

Convergence Speed:
Regarding the convergence speed of the value function (as indicated by the curves),
PMAAR-TD
exhibits a significant acceleration compared to
Single TD
across the majority of agents.

2.

Approximation Accuracy:
In terms of the reward (y-axis), it is evident that
FedTD-Uniform
converges to a suboptimal value in most agent configurations. This discrepancy suggests a lack of adaptability to heterogeneous environments, whereas
PMAAR-TD
maintains consistency with the optimal baseline (single agent).

Evaluation of value functions is relatively simple when the policy is fixed and the corresponding true values are determined. Consequently, we consider control problems, where policies are learned and updated throughout training, leading to increased uncertainty in learning.

6.1.1
Comparison with two-timescale setting

We further evaluate
PMAAR-TD
against the two-timescale reinforcement learning framework (see Figure
2
) within the same fixed-policy Acrobot environment. For this comparison, a global learning rate of
2\times 10^{-4}
is employed, while the critic layers are specifically configured with a learning rate of
2\times 10^{-5}
. The results demonstrate that, consistent with theoretical expectations, the two-timescale approach exhibits significantly slower empirical convergence compared to our single-timescale setting, further validating the efficiency of our proposed synchronization mechanism.

6.2
Application to Control Problems

In this section, we extend
PMAAR-TD
to the actor-critic framework by utilizing
PMAAR-TD
as the critic component and incorporate an additional actor component. The action heads are also distinct across agents in our setting, i.e., agents do not share the parameters of those layers.

6.2.1
Model Design

The architecture of the model employed in our experiments is defined as follows:

\mathbf{s}_{t}
MLP
Actor Head
\pi(a_{t}|s_{t})
Critic Head
V(s_{t})
State
Feature Extraction
Action
Value

The fundamental distinction between
SingleAC
and
FedAC-Uniform
lies in the synchronization mechanism; specifically,
SingleAC
operates independently, whereas both
FedAC-Uniform
and
PMAAR-AC
incorporate periodic synchronization. Furthermore, the key innovation of
PMAAR-AC
is its selective synchronization strategy. During the synchronization phase,
PMAAR-AC
updates only the shared representation layers, while retaining the actor and critic heads as personalized local parameters to preserve agent-specific policies.

6.2.2
Environment Configurations

Regarding the environment configurations, we find that the settings used in prior work are not sufficiently heterogeneous
(
18
;
42
)
, as a single uniform policy can perform well across all environments. We therefore propose a new design that introduces stronger heterogeneity. Specifically, we create a mirrored environment where the agent’s action will be mapped to a reversed/unwanted actual action. When agents operate in normal and mirrored environments respectively, a shared policy cannot be effectively learned, as the optimal behaviors for one group are adversarial to the other.

In our experiments, we employ three distinct random seeds and set the synchronization interval
E={50}
as shared hyperparameters across all configurations. The environment-specific parameters are detailed as follows:

6.2.3
Result

We discuss the results presented in Figure
5
and
5
from three primary perspectives:

1.

Convergence Speed
: In terms of convergence velocity, it is evident that
PMAAR-AC
outperforms both
FedAC-Uniform
and
Single AC
across the majority of agents in both Acrobot and CartPole environments. Compared with
Single AC
, although
PMAAR-AC
exhibits a relatively marginal performance lag during the initial exploration phase, it frequently surpasses the baselines in the mid-training stage and maintains its performance advantage until convergence.

2.

Reward
: Regarding the asymptotic reward obtained post-training,
PMAAR-AC
demonstrates superior performance, outperforming other baselines across the majority of agents. Particularly in the Acrobot environment, the integration of additional data samples facilitated by shared feature synchronization enables the model to converge closer to the theoretical optimum compared to alternative methods.

3.

Training Stability
: Regarding the training stability—represented by the shaded regions reflecting the variance across different seeds—it is evident that for all agents across both environments,
PMAAR-AC
exhibits a consistent narrowing of the confidence intervals as training progresses. Ultimately, our approach achieves the smallest shaded area, indicating significantly lower variance between individual runs and a more robust convergence toward the mean reward curve compared to the baselines.

:
